# 07/08/2026 23:32 — What the Automated Tests Actually Do

Yes — V014 already has **automated test scripts**, and the **100% coverage is real**, but it is important to understand exactly what that proves.

## The simplest picture

When Claude runs:

```powershell
python -m pytest
```

Pytest automatically discovers tests under:

```text
packages/
tests/
```

V014 currently has roughly these test groups:

```text
WhatsApp parser tests       42
Store/provenance tests      34
Architecture/RTM/gate tests 24
──────────────────────────────
Total                       100
                           99 pass
                            1 skip
```

The skip is intentional.

Alongside pytest, `coverage.py` watches which lines of the **implemented production code** execute.

The root configuration explicitly says:

```toml
--cov=at_ingest_core
--cov=at_ingest_whatsapp
--cov=at_store
--cov-fail-under=100
```

So if even **one executable production line** in those three current packages is not exercised, the test command fails.

---

# 1. What does `99 passed` mean?

Each test asks a particular question.

For example, this automated test exists:

```python
def test_basic_android_message():
    ...
```

It gives the parser a WhatsApp line and checks that the correct Event comes out.

Another test:

```python
def test_multiline_message_is_one_event():
```

feeds something like:

```text
07/08/2026, 20:01 - Alice: Hello
This is continuation line
```

and asserts:

```text
1 Event

text =
Hello
This is continuation line
```

If the parser suddenly creates two Events, that test fails automatically.

---

# 2. WhatsApp automation is already reasonably broad

The actual V014 tests cover things such as:

```text
Android format
iOS bracket format
12-hour time
24-hour time
seconds in timestamp
multiline messages
system messages
URLs containing colons
attachments
<Media omitted>
invisible WhatsApp characters
owner flag
unparseable lines
line coverage
stable Event IDs
changed-source Event IDs
invalid hashes
missing workspace
missing origin kind
origin-kind identity
participant deduplication
emoji display names
very long messages
same-timestamp messages
2-digit years
malformed headers
empty exports
long author-like prefixes
```

So there is already significant automated parser testing.

---

# 3. Provenance/store automation

There is another set of tests for the provenance model.

These test things such as:

```text
same Event imported twice → not duplicated

same bytes + two importers
→ 1 registration
→ 2 occurrences

same bytes + different workspaces
→ different registrations

same bytes + different origin_kind
→ different registrations

same timestamp + two importers
→ still 2 occurrences

same importer + same exact instant
→ sequence keeps occurrences separate

broken SourceContent → Registration link
→ rejected

broken Registration → Occurrence link
→ rejected

forged registration ID
→ rejected

forged occurrence ID
→ rejected

revoking one importer
→ another active import still supports Event

revoking final importer
→ Event becomes orphaned

revocation cannot be overwritten by replay

store.durable == False
```

Those are not manual claims. They are executable tests.

---

# 4. Architecture itself is automatically tested

This part is particularly interesting.

There are **24 architecture/gate tests**.

They don't test WhatsApp messages. They inspect the project itself.

For example:

```python
test_dependency_direction_is_downward_only()
```

checks that a lower-level package does not depend upward improperly.

There is:

```python
test_no_package_imports_an_app()
```

which helps preserve:

```text
generic package
      ↑
product app may use it

but

generic package
      ✗
must not import product app
```

There is also:

```python
test_no_domain_leakage_into_shared_packages()
```

to prevent product-specific terms entering generic shared packages.

And:

```python
test_no_import_cycles_between_packages()
```

tries to prevent:

```text
A → B → C → A
```

dependency loops.

So part of the test suite is effectively an **architecture policeman**.

---

# 5. RTM is automatically checked too

You have an RTM — Requirements Traceability Matrix.

Suppose RTM says:

```text
RTM-24
Event resolves to exactly one SourceRegistration
```

There is automation checking that the test name referenced by RTM actually exists:

```python
test_every_rtm_test_name_exists()
```

and:

```python
test_rtm_counts_reconcile()
```

So they are trying to enforce:

```text
Requirement
    ↓
named test
    ↓
actual code behaviour
```

This is good practice.

---

# 6. What exactly is “100% code coverage”?

Here is the critical distinction.

Imagine production code:

```python
def divide(a, b):
    if b == 0:
        return None

    return a / b
```

A test runs:

```python
divide(10, 2)
divide(10, 0)
```

Every executable line runs at least once.

Coverage can therefore report:

```text
100%
```

It means:

> **Every counted executable line was executed by at least one test.**

It does **not** mean:

> Every possible input was tested.

It does not mean:

> Every possible logical combination was tested.

And definitely not:

> The architecture is 100% correct.

---

# 7. V014's 100% applies only to currently coded packages

This is very important.

Coverage currently watches:

```text
at_ingest_core       ✅
at_ingest_whatsapp   ✅
at_store             ✅
```

It cannot cover things that do not exist yet:

```text
at_identity          ❌ no code
at_consent           ❌ no code
at_evidence          ❌ no code
at_questions         ❌ no code
at_commitments       ❌ no code
at_timeline          ❌ no code
AT-LOS API           ❌ no code
AT-LOS product       ❌ no code
```

So:

> **100% coverage of the current 328 statements**

does **not** mean:

> **100% of AT-LOS is tested.**

It means only the small implemented foundation has every counted statement exercised.

---

# 8. Where does `328 statements, 0 missed` come from?

Coverage inspects these three packages.

Conceptually:

```text
at-ingest-core
       +
at-ingest-whatsapp
       +
at-store
       ↓
328 executable statements
```

During pytest:

```text
executed = 328
missed   = 0
```

Therefore:

```text
328 / 328 = 100%
```

And because V014 has:

```toml
--cov-fail-under=100
```

if tomorrow Claude adds:

```python
if something:
    do_something_new()
```

but forgets to create a test that reaches `do_something_new()`, coverage becomes, say:

```text
329 statements
328 executed
1 missed
99.70%
```

Then the build **fails**.

That's the useful part.

---

# 9. But coverage has a big weakness

Consider this wrong function:

```python
def add(a, b):
    return a - b
```

Test:

```python
result = add(10, 5)
```

but suppose the test only executes it and never checks:

```python
assert result == 15
```

The line:

```python
return a - b
```

was executed.

Therefore:

```text
coverage = 100%
```

Yet the function is completely wrong.

That is exactly why **coverage alone is insufficient**.

---

# 10. This is why V014 also has mutation testing

V014 has:

```text
13 mutation regression cases
```

The script is:

```text
tools/mutation_regression.py
```

It intentionally **breaks the code**.

Example.

Correct code contains Event identity including source hash.

Mutation script changes it so the hash disappears.

Conceptually:

```text
CORRECT

workspace
+ source
+ SHA256
+ origin
+ locator
→ Event ID
```

Mutation changes it to something weaker.

Then it runs pytest.

Expected:

```text
test fails
```

If test fails:

```text
MUTANT KILLED ✅
```

If all tests still pass:

```text
MUTANT SURVIVED ❌
```

meaning:

> We thought our tests protected this rule, but they actually don't.

---

# 11. V014's 13 mutants are deliberately chosen historical bugs

This is another important distinction.

It is **not full automatic mutation testing across every expression**.

The script contains 13 manually selected defects based on problems we found during V008–V013 reviews.

For example:

### Mutant

Remove content hash from Event ID.

Expected test:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Calculate:

```text
line coverage = number of events / source lines
```

instead of actual line consumption.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Break store deduplication.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Drop importer from occurrence identity.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Drop `origin_kind` from registration identity.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Accept broken provenance linkage.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Replace actual subject with importer.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Drop `origin_kind` from Event identity.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

### Mutant

Make release checker blind to `-1` sentinel.

Expected:

```text
must fail
```

V014 result:

```text
13 / 13 killed
```

That's good.

---

# 12. But even 13/13 mutation does not mean perfection

Because Claude chose **13 known historical bugs**.

Imagine another unknown bug:

```text
timezone conversion wrong around DST
```

If there isn't:

- a test for it,
- or a mutant representing it,

then:

```text
99 tests may pass
100% coverage may pass
13/13 mutations may pass
```

and the timezone bug may still exist.

This is exactly why our deep reviews kept finding defects after green builds.

---

# 13. There are actually multiple automated gates

I would separate V014 automation like this:

| Gate | What it proves |
|---|---|
| **pytest** | Specified examples/requirements behave as asserted |
| **coverage 100%** | Every counted production statement is executed |
| **architecture tests** | Dependency/layer rules are followed |
| **RTM tests** | Requirements point to actual tests and counts reconcile |
| **mutation regression** | 13 known historic defects would now be detected |
| **ruff** | Static style/lint problems |
| **mypy strict** | Type-contract problems |
| **standalone install** | Each package can install independently |
| **release_check** | Selected release/document facts match expected values |
| **real-export verification** | Reference WhatsApp fixture remains reproducible |

They all test **different dimensions**.

That's why one green number isn't enough.

---

# 14. What is the automatic command?

At the project root:

```powershell
python -m pytest
```

is enough to trigger the coverage requirement because `pyproject.toml` contains:

```toml
addopts = "--cov=at_ingest_core --cov=at_ingest_whatsapp --cov=at_store --cov-report=term-missing --cov-fail-under=100"
```

So you don't even need to type the coverage parameters manually.

---

# 15. What happens automatically internally?

When you execute:

```powershell
python -m pytest
```

roughly this happens:

```text
START
  │
  ├── discover test_*.py
  │
  ├── discover def test_*()
  │
  ├── load production modules
  │
  ├── start coverage measurement
  │
  ├── execute WhatsApp tests
  │
  ├── execute store tests
  │
  ├── execute architecture tests
  │
  ├── record assertions
  │
  ├── record executed production lines
  │
  └── calculate coverage
          │
          ├─ test failed? ─────────► BUILD FAIL
          │
          └─ coverage <100%? ─────► BUILD FAIL
```

Only if everything passes:

```text
99 passed
1 skipped
100% coverage
```

---

# 16. You can manually see exactly which lines weren't covered

If someone adds code and coverage drops, terminal output would look something like:

```text
Name                                  Stmts   Miss   Cover   Missing
-------------------------------------------------------------------
at_ingest_core/__init__.py               55      0    100%
at_ingest_whatsapp/__init__.py          126      1     99%   207
at_store/__init__.py                    148      0    100%
-------------------------------------------------------------------
TOTAL                                   329      1     99%
```

`Missing 207` means:

> line 207 never ran during any automated test.

Then developer writes a test covering that legitimate branch.

---

# 17. But they should NOT write fake tests merely to hit lines

This is a danger with enforcing 100%.

Bad development behaviour:

> Coverage says line 207 missing. Make some useless test that simply executes line 207.

Good behaviour:

> What behaviour does line 207 represent? What requirement/edge case should prove that behaviour?

Then test the **behaviour**, not merely the line.

The second approach maintains meaning.

---

# 18. The architecture tests are not included in the 328 coverage count

Another subtle point.

The **tests themselves** aren't part of the measured 328 statements.

Likewise, generally these tools are not in the coverage target:

```text
release_check.py
release_facts.py
mutation_regression.py
build_release.py
standalone_install_check.py
```

because coverage is configured specifically for:

```text
at_ingest_core
at_ingest_whatsapp
at_store
```

So saying:

> **“V014 codebase is 100% covered”**

would be too broad.

Correct wording is:

> **“The three currently implemented production packages have 100% line coverage.”**

That's an important accuracy distinction.

---

# 19. How I'd improve the automation from here

For V015+, I would keep 100% line coverage but add three stronger dimensions:

### **A. Branch coverage**

Current coverage primarily proves lines.

Example:

```python
if A and B:
    ...
```

A test could execute the line without adequately testing all meaningful true/false combinations.

Enable branch measurement:

```text
branch = true
```

Then report both line and branch coverage.

---

### **B. Property-based testing**

For parser and identity rules, instead of writing only examples:

```text
line 1
line 2
line 3
```

generate hundreds/thousands of variations.

Useful especially for:

```text
timestamps
unicode
display names
multiline data
hash identities
duplicate sequences
```

Hypothesis in Python is good for this.

---

### **C. Real mutation engine eventually**

Current 13 curated mutants are good regression checks.

Later, use broader mutation tooling so the tool automatically tries things like:

```text
== → !=
< → <=
True → False
remove condition
change arithmetic
remove return value
```

Then calculate a mutation score.

Not necessarily right now; curated mutants are appropriate while the code is small.

---

# 20. What manual testing adds that automation doesn't

This links directly to your previous question.

Automation says:

```text
given our predetermined inputs
and predetermined expected behaviours
the code satisfies those assertions
```

Manual testing says:

> **Does this make sense with actual human data and actual usage?**

For example:

```text
real 5-year WhatsApp export
35,000 messages
strange Tamil/English mix
deleted messages
attachments
WhatsApp format changes
unexpected Unicode
```

may reveal cases developers never imagined.

So you need both:

```text
AUTOMATION
    +
REAL MANUAL ACCEPTANCE
    +
INDEPENDENT REVIEW
```

not one instead of the others.

---

# 21. The exact status I would display on the project

Rather than:

> **100% tested ✅**

I would display:

```text
IMPLEMENTED PRODUCTION PACKAGES
────────────────────────────────

at-ingest-core
Line coverage:        100%

at-ingest-whatsapp
Line coverage:        100%

at-store
Line coverage:        100%

Automated tests:      99 passed / 1 skipped
Architecture guards:  24
Historic mutants:     13/13 killed

NOT YET MEASURED
────────────────────────────────

Full branch coverage
General mutation score
Large real-world corpus accuracy
Identity resolution
Consent
Evidence extraction
Product API
End-to-end AT-LOS product
```

That is much harder to misunderstand.

## The key point

**100% coverage currently means every executable line of the three implemented generic Python packages ran during automated testing.**

It does **not** mean 100% of requirements, inputs, architecture, products or AT-LOS have been tested.

The strongest thing V014 currently has is actually the **combination** of 99 passing tests + 100% line coverage + architecture guards + RTM linkage + 13/13 known-defect mutations. But your manual real-data test remains necessary because all of those automated tests are still based on cases the developers already thought to write.

# 07/08/2026 23:58 — V015 Deep Review

Partner, I have now reviewed **the V015 release itself**, not Claude's summary.

I expanded both levels of the ZIP:

- **12 outer release artefacts**
- **49 files inside `at-platform_repo`**
- **61 files total**

I reran the executable suite, inspected the new CLI, ADR-015, architecture manifest, generated FACTS system, release tooling, RTM/architecture guards, current workbooks, and compared the unchanged historical artefacts against V014 by hash.

## Overall verdict

**V015 is materially better than V014, but I would still not call it a clean baseline.**

The new architecture/product separation is directionally correct, the automated test result is stronger, and the generated FACTS approach genuinely solves one recurring class of drift.

But I found **several current-authority contradictions** that the new release-integrity system still misses, plus one important architectural issue around `at-cli` versus future product/API orchestration.

I recommend **V016 correction before starting significant new capability coding**.

---

# 1. First: Claude's stated test numbers are stale

Claude told you:

```text
99 passed, 1 skipped
328 statements
100%
```

That is **not V015's final repository**.

I independently ran the pristine V015 suite:

```text
117 passed
1 skipped

385 statements
0 missed

80 branches
0 partial branches

100% line coverage
100% branch coverage
```

So the package itself is **better than Claude's message**.

Importantly, the actual V015 metadata and generated FACTS blocks contain the correct final numbers:

```text
118 collected
117 passed
1 skipped
43 parser tests
34 store tests
29 architecture guards
385 statements
80 branches
100%
13 mutation regressions
46 RTM rules
```

Therefore this is primarily a **Claude response/reporting error**, not a packaged-facts error.

Still, it is worth recording:

> Release chat summaries must be generated from `release_facts.json` too, rather than manually typed.

Otherwise the ZIP says one thing and Claude announces another.

---

# 2. The generated FACTS solution is genuinely better

This part of V015 is a substantial improvement.

Instead of writing:

```text
39 parser tests
19 store tests
```

in prose and trying to catch stale numbers with regex, V015 generates a marked block:

```text
<!-- FACTS:BEGIN -->

...generated figures...

<!-- FACTS:END -->
```

from one structured fact source.

That is exactly the direction I recommended.

The following current repository documents all carry the same generated figures:

- `README.md`
- `READ_THIS_FIRST.md`
- `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md`
- `docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md`

This removes a large class of previous mistakes.

**Keep this architecture.**

---

# 3. But the FACTS generator itself contains a factual bug

The generated block says:

> **“These figures describe the three implemented production packages only”**

and immediately lists:

```text
at-cli
at-ingest-core
at-ingest-whatsapp
at-store
```

That's **four packages**, not three.

The mistake is hard-coded in:

```text
tools/gen_facts_block.py
```

as:

```python
"These figures describe the three implemented production"
```

while the package list is generated dynamically.

So the newly generated “single truth” mechanism has generated the same wrong statement into **all four authoritative documents**.

### This is a useful lesson

Generation fixes:

> stale copies of a correct fact.

Generation does **not** fix:

> a wrong rule in the generator.

The sentence should itself be derived:

```text
“These figures describe the 4 currently measured production packages”
```

or, better, avoid the count entirely:

> “Coverage applies to the following currently measured packages: …”

---

# 4. `READ_THIS_FIRST.md` directly contradicts V015's coding status

This is a serious onboarding defect.

Earlier in the same document it correctly says:

```text
at-cli — acceptance report
```

and describes it as implemented.

But under **“What does not exist”** it says:

> `at-evidence`, `at-identity`, `at-consent`, ..., **`at-cli`**, `at-ingest-llm-export`, `apps/atlos-api` are specification only.

That is false.

`at-cli` exists and has:

```text
packages/at-cli/
  pyproject.toml
  src/at_cli/
  tests/test_cli.py
```

and contributes to the 117 passing tests.

This is precisely the kind of contradiction `READ_THIS_FIRST` must never contain because a new Claude/ChatGPT/developer is explicitly told:

> “If an AI tells you one of these is built, it is wrong.”

In this case **the document itself is wrong**.

**High severity.**

---

# 5. Project Memory Sprint Tracker is also stale

This is even more important because Project Memory is current authoritative planning.

I found these V015 rows:

### S1-09

```text
CLI: ingest a file, print coverage
State: Planned
```

But V015 has just implemented `at-cli`.

That row should now be Done/Superseded by S1-36 or otherwise reconciled.

---

### S1-19

```text
Mutation testing baseline
State: In Progress
Evidence: 4/4 spot-check killed
```

But V015 has:

```text
13/13 curated mutants
```

The task may legitimately remain In Progress if the target is a full mutation baseline, but the **evidence column is stale** and should clearly distinguish:

```text
curated historical regression: 13/13
full mutation baseline: still pending
```

---

### S2-07

Still says:

> “Any call touching a **non-owner** without a token raises”

That is old pre-ADR-013 terminology.

Consent is now supposed to be:

```text
subject + purpose + authority
```

not:

```text
owner vs non-owner
```

This is current-state drift in the authoritative workbook.

---

# 6. Test Strategy still resurrects the old consent model

Current `docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md` says:

> `require_consent()` raises for any **non-owner subject** without a grant

and later:

> Consent enforcement — **Non-owner processing** raises without a grant

Again, this is the architecture V013 explicitly rejected.

The correct language should be something like:

> Processing requiring authority must fail when no valid authority/grant exists for the relevant subject and purpose.

No importer/owner distinction should determine consent authority.

This is **High severity** because `at-consent` is still not implemented; current specification language will directly influence the code when it is started.

---

# 7. ARCHITECTURE has a stale Event-ID formula

This is a particularly important documentation defect.

The actual V015 code correctly computes:

```python
workspace_ref
+ source_id
+ source_sha256
+ origin_kind
+ locator
```

for `Event.make_id()`.

That is the ADR-014 correction.

But current `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` still documents:

```text
event_id =
sha256(
    workspace_ref |
    source_id |
    source_sha256 |
    locator
)
```

It **omits `origin_kind`**.

That is exactly the V013 identity defect ADR-014 was introduced to fix.

So the executable implementation is correct, but the **authoritative architecture specification describes the old identity**.

This is a **High severity documentation/contract defect**.

---

# 8. Architecture schema version is also stale

`architecture.toml` says:

```text
schema_version = 3
```

but `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` says:

> **Schema version `2`.**

Again, current authoritative document versus machine source.

The release checker doesn't catch it.

---

# 9. `at-cli` manual acceptance exists — but the advertised command doesn't work from a fresh checkout

This is a very practical issue.

V015 tells you:

```bash
python -m at_cli acceptance <whatsapp-export.txt>
```

and advertises this as:

> “Check it yourself, without writing Python.”

Good idea.

But in a freshly extracted repository I ran:

```bash
python -m at_cli --help
```

and got:

```text
No module named at_cli
```

Why?

Because `at-cli` is inside:

```text
packages/at-cli/src/
```

and has not been installed into the environment.

Pytest works because `conftest.py` injects package `src` directories into the Python path.

A normal user shell does not get that magic.

Therefore the manual acceptance command is **not currently a fresh-checkout command**.

You need either:

```text
installation/setup instructions
```

before the command, or a root-level script that deliberately sets up/imports the packages.

For example the user journey could eventually be:

```powershell
python -m venv .venv
.venv\Scripts\activate
pip install -e packages/at-ingest-core
pip install -e packages/at-ingest-whatsapp
pip install -e packages/at-store
pip install -e packages/at-cli

python -m at_cli acceptance myexport.txt
```

or preferably a one-command bootstrap.

Without that, the CLI exists in code but your manual test instructions are incomplete.

---

# 10. The CLI itself is useful and appropriately small

I reviewed the implementation.

It really does orchestrate:

```text
file
 ↓
read_source()
 ↓
SourceContent
SourceRegistration
ImportOccurrence
 ↓
WhatsApp parser
 ↓
Event[]
 ↓
InMemoryEventStore
 ↓
re-ingest
second importer
provenance lookup
revocation
 ↓
PASS / FAIL report
```

And it explicitly ends with:

```text
Nothing is persisted — the store is in-memory.
No identity resolution, evidence or consent exists yet.
```

That is excellent honesty.

The CLI is **not pretending the product exists**.

---

# 11. But the architecture says `at-cli` outputs “persisted records”

`architecture.toml` defines `at_cli.outputs` as:

```text
Terminal output, persisted records
```

That is false today.

The same generated architecture document carries:

> **Out:** Terminal output, persisted records

Yet the CLI itself explicitly says:

> **Nothing is persisted.**

This isn't merely future wording; `at-cli` is marked **Implemented**.

For an Implemented capability its output contract should reflect actual behaviour:

```text
Terminal acceptance report; in-memory orchestration only
```

Persistence can become an output after durable store exists.

---

# 12. Product/application boundary: major improvement

ADR-015 is directionally good.

The machine manifest finally declares:

```text
apps/atlos-api
apps/ipbs-ceaas
```

rather than talking about them only in prose.

That makes the desired direction explicit:

```text
Generic platform package
         ↑
         │ consumed by
         │
Product app
```

and forbids:

```text
generic package → product app
product A → product B
```

That's valuable.

The fact that **IPBS is declared before being coded** is also useful because it tests that AT-LOS isn't accidentally becoming the universal centre of everything.

I support that decision.

---

# 13. But `apps/atlos-api` is allowed to depend on `at-cli`

This deserves another architecture decision.

Current manifest says:

```text
apps/atlos-api
allowed = [
   ...
   at_cli
]
```

I would question that.

`at-cli` is a **delivery/orchestration interface**.

A future HTTP API should normally not be:

```text
AT-LOS HTTP API
      ↓
CLI
      ↓
domain packages
```

because then CLI becomes a hidden application-service layer.

Instead both could depend on a reusable use-case layer:

```text
                ┌── at-cli
                │
use-case layer ─┤
                │
                └── atlos-api
```

or each app could orchestrate shared domain services according to the decision made in DQ-014.

But **DQ-014 is explicitly still open**.

Therefore declaring:

```text
atlos-api may import at-cli
```

looks premature.

I'd remove `at_cli` from the product application's allowed dependencies until DQ-014 is resolved.

---

# 14. Related issue: `at-cli` is allowed to import almost everything

Current architecture allows `at_cli` to consume:

```text
core
whatsapp
llm-export
store
identity
consent
evidence
questions
commitments
timeline
```

That makes it a potentially enormous **god-orchestrator**.

For today's acceptance command, it needs only:

```text
core
WhatsApp
store
```

It may eventually need more—but authorising all future dependencies now weakens the architecture boundary.

A safer principle is:

> **Allowed dependency lists describe what code needs now, not everything it might someday need.**

Then extend the allow-list only alongside the feature requiring it.

---

# 15. Product-state tests are useful, but one advertised guard still skips

The suite has:

```text
29 architecture guards collected
28 passed
1 skipped
```

The skip remains:

```text
test_no_package_imports_an_app
```

because no actual `apps/` directories exist yet.

This is not a problem by itself.

But wording like:

> product boundary fully tested

needs nuance.

Today what is tested is mainly:

```text
manifest-level product declaration/invariants
```

Actual AST/import scanning against product source code cannot happen until an app directory exists.

That's perfectly acceptable—but call it what it is.

---

# 16. Mutation harness still has a dangerous checkout-corruption property

This happened during my review.

I ran:

```text
python tools/mutation_regression.py
```

The process exceeded my execution timeout and was externally killed.

After that, the working repository was **left mutated**.

A subsequent release check caused the suite to fail with exactly the identity defect the mutant had introduced.

I then discarded that working copy and re-extracted the pristine V015 ZIP.

So:

- pristine V015 was good before mutation;
- **my mutation run corrupted the checkout when killed externally**.

Claude already recognised in V014 that `try/finally` cannot survive SIGKILL.

The V015 preflight detects an already-corrupted target **before a new run**, but it does not make the original mutation atomic.

This remains a tooling risk.

### Better long-term solution

Don't mutate the working tree directly.

Run every mutant in:

```text
temporary copy
git worktree
temporary checkout
```

and destroy it afterward.

Then even:

```text
SIGKILL
machine crash
terminal closure
CI cancellation
```

cannot damage the developer's real source tree.

I would make that a V016 tooling task.

---

# 17. Release integrity still does not catch semantic workbook drift

This is now very clear.

The package audit can read workbooks and look for:

- superseded terms,
- versions,
- selected facts.

Yet it missed the current Project Memory contradictions I listed:

```text
S1-09 at-cli still Planned
S1-19 old 4/4 evidence
S2-07 non-owner consent wording
```

So:

> `release integrity 0 failures`

still means:

> all **currently implemented audit rules** passed.

It does **not** mean:

> every current fact in every artefact is correct.

That phrase should be used carefully.

>

> - S1-09 still says CLI Planned although at-cli exists/S1-36 is Done.
> - S1-19 still carries 4/4 mutation evidence; distinguish 13/13 curated regression from the still-pending full mutation baseline.
> - S2-07 still says “non-owner”; replace owner semantics with subject + purpose + required authority.
>
> **4. Repair Test Strategy consent language.** It still specifies “non-owner subject” and “non-owner processing”. ADR-013/015 explicitly separated importer provenance from consent subject/authority.
>
> **5. Repair authoritative Event identity documentation.** `docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` still documents `event_id = sha256(workspace|source|hash|locator)` and omits `origin_kind`, while the code correctly includes it under ADR-014. Generate this identity formula from a structured identity definition or test the exact documented components.
>
> **6. Repair architecture schema-version drift.** Manifest schema is 3; ARCHITECTURE says schema 2.
>
> **7. Fix at-cli's declared output.** `architecture.toml` says “Terminal output, persisted records” although V015 explicitly has no persistence. For current Implemented behaviour it should say terminal acceptance report/in-memory orchestration. Durable output belongs after S1-20.
>
> **8. Make the advertised manual command executable from a clean checkout.** `python -m at_cli acceptance ...` currently fails with `No module named at_cli` unless packages have already been installed or pytest's conftest altered `sys.path`. Supply a documented/bootstrap installation path and add a true clean-checkout CLI acceptance test.

> **9. Revisit DQ-014 boundary before allowing atlos-api → at-cli.** The application/use-case layer is explicitly undecided, yet `apps/atlos-api.allowed` already includes `at_cli`. A future network API should not accidentally depend on a CLI delivery layer. Remove this until DQ-014 resolves orchestration placement.

> **10. Tighten at-cli's allowed dependency set.** It currently may import every planned downstream package. Allowed dependencies should represent current necessary dependencies, not hypothetical future permission. Expand alongside implementation.
>
> **11. Make mutation testing crash-safe.** V015 still mutates the real working tree. An externally killed mutation run left my review checkout mutated. Run each mutant in a temporary copy/git worktree so SIGKILL/CI cancellation cannot contaminate source.
>

# 1. HIGH — RTM-49 does **not** actually prove the advertised clean-checkout workflow

Claude says:

> `bootstrap.py` because `python -m at_cli` failed from a clean checkout; RTM-49 now runs it in a clean subprocess.

And RTM-49 says:

> **“The advertised CLI command runs in a clean environment.”**

But the test does this:

```python
src = [
    "packages/at-ingest-core/src",
    "packages/at-ingest-whatsapp/src",
    "packages/at-store/src",
    "packages/at-cli/src",
]

env = {
    "PYTHONPATH": ":".join(src),
}
```

Then it runs:

```text
python -m at_cli ...
```

That proves:

> CLI works if a test manually injects all source directories into `PYTHONPATH`.

It does **not prove**:

```text
fresh checkout
    ↓
python bootstrap.py
    ↓
packages really installed
    ↓
python -m at_cli acceptance ...
```

### Worse, the standalone package test does not close this gap

When the CLI tests are copied into the standalone clean venv, RTM-49 does:

```python
if root is None:
    pytest.skip(...)
```

So the standalone environment **skips the very test supposedly proving clean-environment CLI execution**.

And RTM-50 only tests:

```text
bootstrap.py --check
```

reporting missing packages.

It does not test successful installation.

### I manually tried a genuinely fresh venv

I ran V016's:

```text
python bootstrap.py
```

from a fresh temporary venv.

It failed at the first package because this sandbox could not obtain:

```text
hatchling
```

That specific failure is an **environment/package-index limitation**, so I do **not** mark bootstrap itself as broken.

But it confirms the important distinction:

**the actual bootstrap-install workflow has not been automated end-to-end.**

### Fix

Create a real integration test/script:

```text
create temporary venv
    ↓
run bootstrap.py using that venv's python
    ↓
assert four packages import
    ↓
run python -m at_cli acceptance sample.txt
    ↓
assert RESULT: PASS
```

That can run in CI where package installation/network is available.

RTM-49 should not be PASSING until that test exists.

---

# 2. HIGH — RTM-47 says “exactly”, but only checks half the claim

This is subtle but architecturally important.

RTM-47 says:

> **“Documented identity components genuinely change the computed id”**

and the test docstring goes further:

> “Every documented component must CHANGE the id when varied, and **the set must be complete**.”

What does the implementation actually test?

For `event_id`, it checks:

```text
workspace_ref changes → id changes
source_id changes     → id changes
source_sha256 changes → id changes
origin_kind changes   → id changes
locator changes       → id changes
```

Good.

But it does **not** prove:

> those are the **only** inputs used by the implementation.

A future developer could add an undocumented dependency to `Event.make_id()` and every existing assertion could continue passing.

So this:

> “exactly its documented components”

is stronger than what the test establishes.

### Better test

Construct the **expected ID directly from `identity.toml`** and compare:

```python
expected_key = "|".join(
    value_of(component)
    for component in identity_spec["components"]
)

expected = sha256(expected_key)[:16]

assert Event.make_id(ref) == expected
```

Then the structured definition becomes the executable specification.

---

# 3. HIGH — `identity.toml` defines four identities, but only `event_id` is tied back to implementation

V016 introduces:

```text
identity.toml
```

with:

1. `event_id`
2. `registration_id`
3. `occurrence_id`
4. `person_id`

This is a good direction.

But RTM-47 tests only:

```text
event_id
```

There is no architecture test iterating over:

```text
registration_id
occurrence_id
```

and asserting the implementation uses exactly their documented components.

Current unit tests protect some of these behaviours independently, but that is different from proving:

```text
identity.toml
      ⇅
actual implementation
```

For example `identity.toml` says:

```text
registration_id:
workspace_ref
source_id
content_sha256
origin_kind
```

and:

```text
occurrence_id:
registration_id
importer_ref
imported_at
sequence
```

Those definitions should be asserted generically.

Otherwise V016 has created a **single structured identity registry**, but only one quarter of it is truly machine-linked.

### Fix

Generalise RTM-47 into something like:

```text
test_every_implemented_identity_matches_identity_toml
```

`person_id` should explicitly skip because `at-identity` is Planned.

---

# 4. MEDIUM/HIGH — current Test Strategy still says implemented `at-cli` is Outstanding

This is a straightforward current-document defect.

`docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md` says:

> **Outstanding:** `at-ingest-llm-export` (≥12 tests), **`at-cli`** (orchestration — the only adapter consumer), S1-20 durable persistence.

But V016 clearly has:

```text
at-cli — Implemented
14 CLI tests
manual acceptance command
```

The same document's generated FACTS block lists `at-cli` as a measured production package.

So the document contradicts itself.

This also proves again that:

> `release integrity 0 failures`

does **not** mean every current semantic statement is checked.

---

# 5. MEDIUM — Project Memory's V015 historical snapshot has contradictory labelling

In `10_HallucinationRegister`, V016 now correctly has:

> **CURRENT COUNT (V016 - the only current snapshot)**

Good.

But the previous row was renamed:

> **HISTORICAL SNAPSHOT (V015 - the only current snapshot)**

Those two phrases conflict:

```text
HISTORICAL SNAPSHOT
vs
the only current snapshot
```

It is clearly meant to preserve what was said in V015, but once it is made historical the phrase should become:

> “HISTORICAL SNAPSHOT — V015; was current in V015”

rather than continuing to call itself current.

Small defect, but this register exists specifically to stop historical facts masquerading as current facts.

---

# 6. MEDIUM — Project Memory Standard still overstates release-check coverage

The V016 Standard says:

> `tools/release_check.py` **asserts every current document**

That is too broad.

We already have a current example that escaped it:

```text
docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md
→ calls at-cli Outstanding
```

The release check doesn't understand every semantic statement in a document.

I'd change this to:

> "`release_check.py` asserts defined machine-verifiable current-document invariants and generated fact blocks."

That is accurate.

---

# 7. MEDIUM — the advertised bootstrap path has an undeclared operational prerequisite

The user-facing instructions now say simply:

```text
python bootstrap.py
python -m at_cli acceptance <export>
```

`bootstrap.py` performs editable pip installs, and all packages use:

```toml
[build-system]
requires = ["hatchling"]
```

So on a genuinely bare environment it needs access to a package index or preinstalled/cached build tooling.

That is normal Python packaging—not an architecture problem.

But because this command is now being presented as the **human acceptance entry point**, the onboarding should say something like:

```text
Requires Python 3.11+ and pip access to install hatchling.
```

Otherwise a user with a locked-down/offline system sees bootstrap fail before reaching AT-LOS code and may think the product is broken.

---

# 8. Mutation-harness crash-safety — V016 fix is real

This one I specifically tested because V015/V014 had hurt us repeatedly.

V016 now does:

```text
real repository
     ↓ copy
temporary repository
     ↓
mutate temp source
     ↓
run tests
     ↓
destroy temp copy
```

I recorded hashes of the real source files, launched the mutation harness under an external timeout, killed it, and compared the source afterward.

**The real source remained unchanged.**

So this is a genuine architectural/tooling correction.

✅ Accept.

---

# 9. ADR-016 orchestration decision — I agree with it

The dependency direction now looks much healthier.

Current `at-cli`:

```text
at-cli
 ├── at-ingest-core
 ├── at-ingest-whatsapp
 └── at-store
```

Future AT-LOS product:

```text
apps/atlos-api
 ├── shared domain packages
 └── NOT at-cli
```

That prevents:

```text
HTTP API
   ↓
CLI
   ↓
business/domain code
```

which would have been the wrong layering.

I agree with:

> **A delivery mechanism should not become the application layer by accident.**

And I agree with keeping **DQ-014 open** until there is a second genuine orchestration consumer.

---

# 10. One thing I would watch: `Participant.matches_declared_export_identity`

This was renamed from `is_owner`, which was definitely the right move.

But it still lives in the **generic core `Participant` contract** while its own documentation says:

> “Adapter-local fact ONLY.”

Conceptually:

```text
WhatsApp adapter-local parse fact
       ↓
generic Participant
       ↓
generic Event
       ↓
store/downstream
```

So the value physically travels beyond the adapter even though policy says it should not be interpreted downstream.

Currently there is a regression proving it doesn't enter `SubjectRef`, which is good.

I wouldn't block V016 on this, but before adding multiple adapters I'd ask:

> Should adapter-local parse annotations live in the generic canonical Participant at all?

This may become clearer once LLM/email adapters exist.

---

# 11. What is genuinely coded at V016

The current state remains:

| Capability | Status |
|---|---|
| `at-ingest-core` | ✅ Implemented |
| `at-ingest-whatsapp` | ✅ Implemented |
| `at-store` | 🟡 Partial, memory only |
| `at-cli` | ✅ Implemented |
| `at-ingest-llm-export` | ❌ Planned |
| `at-identity` | ❌ Planned |
| `at-consent` | ❌ Planned |
| `at-evidence` | ❌ Planned |
| questions/commitments/timeline | ❌ Planned |
| AT-LOS product | ❌ Not coded |
| network/HTTP API | ❌ Not coded |
| persistent database | ❌ Not coded |
| UI | ❌ Not coded |

This representation in V016 is now quite good.

# What I would send Claude for V017

> **V016 deep review complete. V016 is materially stronger and the principal V015 fixes are real, but do one V017 integrity pass before expanding capability coding.**
>
> **1. RTM-49 currently overclaims clean-checkout verification.** `test_cli_is_runnable_as_a_module_from_a_clean_environment` manually injects all four source trees through `PYTHONPATH`. That proves source-tree execution, not `bootstrap.py → installed packages → python -m at_cli`. In the standalone copied test the case skips because `_repo_root()` is absent. RTM-50 only verifies bootstrap's missing-package report.
>
> Add a true CI integration test using a temporary venv:
>
> `create venv → run bootstrap.py using that venv → import all four packages → python -m at_cli acceptance fixture → RESULT PASS`.
>
> Only then call RTM-49 “advertised CLI command runs from a clean checkout.”
>
> **2. Strengthen RTM-47.** Its test proves every *listed* event-id component influences the id, but does not prove there are no undocumented inputs. Calculate the expected deterministic ID directly from the ordered `identity.toml` components and compare it to `Event.make_id()`.
>
> **3. Apply the structured identity contract to every implemented identity.** `identity.toml` defines `event_id`, `registration_id`, `occurrence_id`, and future `person_id`; RTM-47 currently validates only event_id against implementation. Add a generic identity-contract test for event, registration and occurrence; explicitly mark person_id unimplemented.
>
> **4. Fix current TEST_STRATEGY drift.** Its Sprint-1 paragraph still lists `at-cli` as Outstanding even though V016 implements and measures it. This must fail a current-capability audit.
>
> **5. Fix the Hallucination Register's V015 historical snapshot wording.** It currently says `HISTORICAL SNAPSHOT (V015 - the only current snapshot)`. Once historical, it must no longer describe itself as current.
>
> **6. Narrow the Project Memory Standard claim.** `release_check.py` does not “assert every current document” semantically; the current at-cli Outstanding defect proves that. Say it asserts defined machine-verifiable document invariants/generated facts.
>
> **7. Document bootstrap prerequisites.** `python bootstrap.py` requires Python/pip plus availability of the package build backend (`hatchling`). Keep the one-command path, but state the prerequisite and make CI the authoritative successful-bootstrap evidence.
>
> **8. Keep the V016 mutation-isolation design.** I externally interrupted the harness and verified the real source tree hashes did not change. This fix is accepted.
>
> **9. Do not start `at-consent`; DQ-012/DQ-013 remain legitimate blockers. Do not create FastAPI/product HTTP handlers until DQ-014 is settled by an actual second orchestration consumer.**
>
> Suggested package:
>
> `AT-LifeOperatingSystem_ExecutableIdentityAndBootstrapProof_Claude_V017_<YYYYMMDD_HHMM>.zip`

# Final verdict

**V016 executable code:** **PASS**

**100% line/branch coverage claim:** **PASS — independently reproduced**

**Manifest integrity:** **PASS**

**ADR-016 product/orchestration boundary:** **PASS**

**Mutation crash-safety:** **PASS — independently spot-tested**

**Structured identity design:** **GOOD, but only partially enforcement-linked**

**Clean-checkout acceptance claim:** **NOT YET PROVEN END-TO-END**

**Current documentation:** **one clear stale `at-cli` statement + smaller authority wording issues**

### Baseline decision

I would call V016 **technically usable but not yet freeze it as the final foundation**. V017 should be a fairly small correction release. After those corrections, I would be much more comfortable allowing work on `at-ingest-llm-export` / durable store / identity rather than continuing another large architecture rewrite.

Think of the tools like this:

| Tool | Typical strength |

No.

# A new issue I want corrected

There is one architectural wording inconsistency worth cleaning up before proceeding.

`docs/ARCHITECTURE.md` says:

> **“All IDs are deterministic content hashes, never sequences”**

But immediately afterward `occurrence_id` is described as:

```text
processing-run (not reproducible)
components:
registration_id
importer_ref
imported_at
sequence
```

The hash itself is deterministic **given those inputs**, but `occurrence_id` represents a deliberately non-reproducible import occurrence and includes a sequence.

So:

> “All IDs are deterministic content hashes, never sequences”

is too broad.

Better:

> **All IDs are hash-encoded. Source-derived identities are deterministic and reproducible. Processing-occurrence identity deliberately includes occurrence-specific inputs such as timestamp and sequence.**

This matters because otherwise another developer might interpret “never sequences” as meaning the sequence field is architecturally prohibited.

### Severity: Medium

---

# Another small but useful improvement

The V017 integration test has:

```python
shutil.rmtree(venv, ignore_errors=True)
```

inside a:

```python
with tempfile.TemporaryDirectory()
```

The explicit `rmtree` is redundant because the temporary directory cleans itself up.

Not a defect. I would simply remove it eventually to keep the integration test minimal.

---

# Now your new requirement — I strongly agree with it

What exists today is **testing implementation**, but not yet a proper **testing product/documentation architecture**.

At present testing is spread across:

```text
tests/
packages/*/tests/
docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md
TestStrategy.xlsx
READ_THIS_FIRST.md
at-cli acceptance
CI
mutation tool
standalone installer
```

An engineer can understand it after studying the repository.

A new tester or user **cannot**.

And as this system grows, we will forget:

- which tests are developer-only,
- which tests a customer/user may run,
- what test data is safe,
- how to manually verify a feature,
- what each script is for,
- what is automated versus manual,
- what is unit/integration/system/acceptance/security/performance,
- what should never be exposed externally.

So I agree with you: **build that structure now while the product is small.**

---

# I would separate testing into TWO worlds

## 1. INTERNAL testing

For:

```text
Developers
QA engineers
architects
release engineers
support engineers
AI coding agents
```

This contains things customers should normally never need to see.

Examples:

```text
unit tests
architecture guards
mutation tests
RTM verification
CI internals
release-integrity tests
identity-contract tests
test fixtures
debugging
fault injection
developer manual tests
performance profiling
security tests
```

---

# 2. EXTERNAL testing

For:

```text
Customer
administrator
implementation partner
demo user
client QA/UAT team
auditor where appropriate
```

Examples:

```text
installation verification
health check
sample import
user acceptance testing
expected screen/output
business workflow checks
API checks later
troubleshooting
known limitations
```

The customer should **not** need to learn pytest, mutation testing or architecture TOMLs to prove the product works.

---

# Recommended repository architecture

I would ask Claude to introduce something like this **before much more capability coding**:

```text
at-platform/
│
├── docs/
│   │
│   ├── architecture/
│   │   ├── 00-index.md
│   │   ├── 01-platform-overview.md
│   │   ├── 02-layer-model.md
│   │   ├── 03-package-boundaries.md
│   │   ├── 04-product-boundaries.md
```

I would **not necessarily move all existing package unit tests immediately** because package-local tests are standard and sensible.

For example:

```text
packages/at-store/tests/
```

should probably remain.

```text
cross-package
integration
architecture
system
manual
external/UAT
```

testing.

---

# Every manual test needs a numbered test document

This directly addresses what you asked.

For example:

```text
tests/internal/manual/
```

could contain:

```text
00000_README.md

00100_GenericEventModel/
    00101_PURPOSE.md
    00102_manual_test.py
    00103_expected_output.txt

00200_WhatsAppIngestion/
    00201_PURPOSE.md
    00202_sample_export.txt
    00203_manual_test.py
    00204_expected_output.txt

00300_Provenance/
    00301_PURPOSE.md
    00302_manual_provenance_test.py
    00303_expected_output.txt

00400_EndToEndAcceptance/
    00401_PURPOSE.md
    00402_sample_export.txt
    00403_run_acceptance.ps1
    00404_expected_output.txt
```

But where automation already exists, don't create Python scripts simply to duplicate production tests.

For example V017 already gives us:

```text
python -m at_cli acceptance <file>
```

So manual-test documentation should often be:

```text
PURPOSE
PRECONDITIONS
COMMAND
TEST DATA
EXPECTED OUTPUT
FAIL CONDITIONS
WHAT IT PROVES
WHAT IT DOES NOT PROVE
TROUBLESHOOTING
```

rather than another script.

That is the structure I used when I manually explained the three current capabilities to you.

---

# Example: manual test document

A file such as:

```text
tests/internal/manual/
00300_Provenance/
00301_PURPOSE.md
```

should read conceptually:

```text
Test ID:
MT-PROV-001

Title:
Same source imported by two importers

Purpose:
Verify source content, source registration and import
occurrence remain separate concepts.

Preconditions:
Platform bootstrap completed.
Sample export available.

Input:
sample_whatsapp.txt

Steps:
1. Import as importer-A.
2. Import exact same bytes as importer-B.
3. Inspect source-content count.
4. Inspect registration count.
5. Inspect occurrence count.
6. Inspect Event count.

Expected:
Content versions        1
Source registrations    1
Import occurrences      2
Events                  unchanged

Failure:
Two registrations appear for identical source version.
Two imports collapse into one occurrence.
Events duplicate.

What this proves:
Import provenance semantics.

What this DOES NOT prove:
Consent.
Identity resolution.
Persistent storage.
```

That last section is especially important.

It stops users from interpreting a successful test as proof of something it does not test.

---

# External tests should be much simpler

Customer-facing UAT should look like SaaS testing, for example:

### Test EXT-UAT-001 — Verify installation

```text
Action:
Open System Health.

Expected:
All required services show Healthy.
```

### EXT-UAT-002 — Import WhatsApp export

```text
Action:
Upload sample export.

Expected:
182 events found.
0 parser issues.
Coverage 100%.
```

The external user should **not** see:

```text
SourceRegistration.make_id()
mutation killer
RTM-47
pytest branch coverage
architecture guard
```

unless they're using a developer/auditor portal.

---

# Your request for a web-page user guide is also correct

I would **not** build one giant README.

From the beginning, create a **documentation site**.

For this stage I would use something simple such as **MkDocs** rather than building a custom React documentation system.

Source remains:

```text
.md
```

but the user gets a navigation-based website:

```text
Home
├── Getting Started
├── User Guide
│   ├── Importing Data
│   ├── Reading Results
│   └── Troubleshooting
├── Testing
│   ├── Internal
│   └── External / UAT
├── Architecture
├── Developer Guide
└── Release Notes
```

Later the public SaaS site could expose only:

```text
Getting Started
User Guide
Admin Guide
API Guide
Troubleshooting
Release Notes
```

while the internal build includes:

```text
Architecture
Developer Guide
Internal Testing
Mutation Testing
RTM
Release Engineering
```

Same source repository, **two publication profiles**.

That is much cleaner than trying to hide sections of one huge help document.

---

# Testing architecture should also be machine-readable

Because we've learned what happens when important architecture exists only in prose.

I would add something like:

```text
testing.toml
```

Eventually it could define:

```text
test area
level
internal/external
automated/manual
blocking/non-blocking
owner
script/location
requirements
CI job
data classification
```

Example:

```text
[[test_suite]]
id = "TST-INT-ARCH"
name = "Architecture guards"
audience = "internal"
type = "automated"
level = "architecture"
blocking = true
path = "tests/internal/architecture"
ci_job = "architecture"

[[test_suite]]
id = "TST-EXT-UAT"
name = "Customer acceptance"
audience = "external"
type = "manual"
level = "acceptance"
blocking = false
path = "tests/external/acceptance"
```

Then the testing documentation index can be **generated from that**, exactly like the architecture and identity sections.

---

# Testing levels I want established now

I would formalise this hierarchy:

| Level | Purpose | Current |
|---|---|---|
| **L0 Static** | lint/types/schema validation | ✅ |
| **L1 Unit** | one class/function/package | ✅ |
| **L2 Contract** | public contracts/identity/interfaces | ✅ partial |
| **L3 Integration** | multiple packages/environment | ✅ now bootstrap integration |
| **L4 Architecture** | dependency/product/layer rules | ✅ |
| **L5 System** | complete running backend | ❌ |
| **L6 Internal manual** | developer/QA exploratory validation | 🟡 informal |
| **L7 External UAT** | customer/admin acceptance | ❌ |
| **L8 Security** | auth, secrets, abuse, tenant isolation | ❌ |
| **L9 Performance** | load, latency, resource usage | ❌ |
| **L10 UI/E2E** | browser/product journey | ❌ |

The important thing is that **missing test levels stay explicitly missing**.

Don't create empty directories just to make it look complete.

---

# Manual testing should have two classifications

Within both internal and external, classify manual tests further:

```text
MANDATORY RELEASE ACCEPTANCE
OPTIONAL EXPLORATORY
```

Example:

```text
internal/manual/release/
```

for tests that must be run before a release.

And:

```text
internal/manual/exploratory/
```

for investigation.

Similarly external:

```text
external/uat/release/
external/uat/customer-specific/
```

That becomes important once you have customers.

---

# Don't put real personal data in external documentation

The current real consultant export result:

```text
283 lines
182 events
```

is useful internal verification evidence.

But future external user guides and sample testing should use **synthetic data**.

So:

```text
tests/internal/fixtures/private/
```

should not be committed if it contains actual data.

And:

```text
tests/external/sample-data/
```

should contain only:

```text
synthetic
anonymised
licensed test data
```

This needs to become a formal test-data rule.

---

I would send Claude this — including your new requirement.

> **V017 deep review substantially passes the V016 corrections. Before expanding platform capability, prepare V018 to establish the testing/documentation architecture so manual, automated, internal and external testing do not become another unstructured layer.**
>
> **1. Preserve V017's identity enforcement and real-venv bootstrap integration.** These corrections are accepted.
>
> **2. Correct the architecture wording “All IDs are deterministic content hashes, never sequences.”** `occurrence_id` is a hash-encoded processing-run identity containing `imported_at` and `sequence`; it is intentionally non-reproducible. Distinguish reproducible source-derived IDs from occurrence/run IDs.
>
> ---
>
> ## TESTING ARCHITECTURE — NEW REQUIREMENT
>
> **3. Create an explicit Testing Architecture document and repository structure.** Testing must be separated by audience and purpose rather than accumulated under one folder.
>

>   external/
> ```
>
> Internal = developer/QA/release/architecture/security/performance tests.
>
> External = customer/admin/UAT/smoke/installation/business acceptance.
>
> **4. Add `docs/testing/internal/07-manual-testing` or equivalent.** Use numbered pages/tests. For every manual test specify:
>
> - Test ID
> - title
> - purpose

> - prerequisite

> - exact steps
> - command/script
> - expected output
> - pass criteria
> - fail criteria
> - cleanup
> - what the test proves

> - related RTM/ADR
> - automation equivalent, if any
>
> Use the existing Generic Event Model, WhatsApp Ingestion, Provenance and full `at-cli acceptance` flow as the first documented manual tests.
>
> **5. Organise root testing by area.** Keep package-local unit tests where appropriate, but root tests should explicitly represent cross-package categories such as:
>
> ```text
> tests/
>   internal/
>     integration/
>     architecture/
>     contract/
>     manual/
>     mutation/
>     security/
>     performance/
>     fixtures/

>     smoke/
>     acceptance/
>     sample-data/
>     expected-results/
> ```
>
> Do not create empty folders representing capabilities that do not exist. The Testing Architecture may declare them Planned.
>
> **6. Define the testing taxonomy.** At minimum distinguish:
>
> - static analysis
> - unit
> - contract
> - integration
> - architecture
> - system
> - internal manual
> - external/UAT
> - security
> - performance
> - UI/E2E
>
> Record state for each: Implemented / Partial / Planned / Blocked.
>
> **7. Introduce `testing.toml` or equivalent machine-readable testing manifest.** For each test suite define:
>
> ```text
> id
> name
> audience = internal|external
> mode = automated|manual
> level
> blocking
> source path
> CI job
> requirement/RTM
> data classification
> state
> ```
>
> Generate the testing index from this manifest. Do not create another prose-only architecture that can drift.
>
> ---
>
> ## DEVELOPER + USER DOCUMENTATION
>
> **8. Split the present documentation into a proper documentation information architecture.** Add:
>
> ```text
> docs/architecture/
> docs/development/
> docs/testing/
> docs/user-guide/
> ```
>
> Architecture explains system design.
>
> Development explains how engineers build/extend/release it.
>
> Testing explains how each verification layer works.
>
> User Guide explains how someone operates the product.
>
> **9. User Guide must have internal and external editions.**
>
> ```text
> docs/user-guide/internal/
> docs/user-guide/external/
> ```
>
> Internal = developers, QA, support, release/admin operations.
>
> External = customers/users/admins/implementation partners.
>
> Never expose internal mutation/architecture/release mechanics as normal customer instructions.
>
> **10. Build documentation as a navigable web site rather than one huge help document.** Use a docs-as-code web generator such as MkDocs unless there is a documented reason to choose another framework.
>
> Provide navigation resembling a SaaS documentation portal:
>
> ```text
> Home
> Getting Started
> User Guide
> Admin Guide
> Testing / UAT
> Troubleshooting
> Architecture       [internal build]
> Developer Guide    [internal build]
> Release Engineering [internal build]

>
> Support **internal and external publication profiles** from the same governed source.
>
> **11. External testing/user-guide examples must use synthetic/anonymised test data.** Real consultant/customer exports are internal verification evidence and must never become public sample fixtures.
>
> **12. Add a Testing Architecture section to the Development Design/Architecture documentation.** It must show:
>
> ```text
> production capability
>      ↓
> unit/contract tests
>      ↓
> integration tests
>      ↓
> system/internal acceptance
>      ↓
> external UAT
>      ↓
> release evidence
> ```
>
> Include the relationship between RTM → automated test → manual test → CI/release evidence.
>
> **13. Add a test-purpose index.** A new engineer should be able to open one page and answer:
>
> - what test suites exist?
> - why does each exist?
> - where is its script?
> - who runs it?
> - when is it run?
> - internal or external?
> - automated or manual?
> - blocking or advisory?
> - what requirement does it prove?
>
> **14. Preserve the rule: a test must not claim more than it proves.** Apply this also to manual and external/UAT tests.
>
> Do not start product HTTP/UI implementation merely to create documentation examples. Document current capability honestly and mark future pages Planned.
>
> Suggested release name:
>
> **`AT-LifeOperatingSystem_TestingArchitectureAndDocumentationSystem_Claude_V018_<YYYYMMDD_HHMM>.zip`**

# Should V018 include actual HTML pages?

I would **not manually maintain both Markdown and HTML**.

The correct model is:

```text
Markdown source
      ↓
documentation generator
      ↓
HTML website
```

Then you get proper SaaS-style webpages without duplicate content.

For example:

```text
docs/
mkdocs.yml
```

and:

```text
python -m mkdocs serve
```

would open something like:

```text
http://localhost:8000/
```

later.

The **source remains reviewable in Git**, while humans get a website.

---

# This will also help Claude Code later

Once Claude Code starts working directly against the repository, one page such as:

```text
docs/development/00-index.md
```

can tell the coding agent:

```text
architecture rules
package structure
test requirements
manual acceptance requirements
how to add RTM
how to update docs
how to run release gates
```

Then Claude Chat doesn't have to repeatedly explain the repository to Claude Code.

The same would help Codex or a human developer.

## My recommendation

**V017 is the first release in several iterations where I would say the core foundation itself is reasonably sound.**

I would **not spend V018 finding another 20 ways to check the same four packages**.

Use V018 to build the **testing architecture + internal/external manual testing + developer/user documentation system** you just described. Then, when new packages begin arriving, every feature will enter an organised framework instead of us trying to reconstruct documentation and testing conventions later.

# Overall verdict

V018 is a **very good structural improvement**. Your request for:

- internal vs external testing,
- manual test procedures,
- numbered test documents,
- user/developer documentation,
- testing architecture,
- machine-readable testing manifest,

has been implemented in a sensible direction.

However, I found **one critical documentation-publication design defect** that means V018 must **not** publish the external documentation site as currently configured.

There are also several smaller but real current-document defects.

## V018 needs V019 before calling the documentation system safe.

---

# 1. BLOCKER — the external MkDocs site does NOT actually exclude internal pages

This is the biggest V018 finding.

Claude says:

> `mkdocs-external.yml` publishes only customer-facing pages.

ADR-018 says:

> Internal material is excluded by **not being navigated to**.

And the guard RTM-62 checks only whether internal paths appear in the external `nav:` section.

That assumption is wrong for MkDocs.

MkDocs explicitly documents that Markdown files which are not listed in `nav` are **still rendered into the built site**. They are simply absent from the navigation menu. citeturn361905search2turn361905search1

So this V018 external profile:

```yaml
docs_dir: docs

nav:
  - Getting started: ...
  - User guide: ...
```

does **not** mean:

```text
only these pages get published
```

It means:

```text
all docs under docs/ are built
but only these appear in navigation
```

That means pages such as:

```text
docs/ARCHITECTURE.md
docs/TEST_STRATEGY.md
docs/architecture/*
docs/development/*
docs/testing/internal/*
docs/user-guide/internal/*
docs/adr/*
```

can still be present in the generated customer-facing site and reachable by direct URL, and depending on search configuration may also be discoverable through search.

This defeats the exact internal/external separation you asked for.

### Why RTM-62 passes

The test only does this:

```python
nav = external.split("nav:", 1)[1]

assert "architecture/" not in nav
assert "development/" not in nav
...
```

It proves:

> internal pages aren't in the navigation menu.

It does **not** prove:

> internal pages aren't built/published.

Those are completely different claims.

### Correct fix

Use MkDocs' actual exclusion mechanism, for example:

```yaml
exclude_docs: |
  ARCHITECTURE.md
  TEST_STRATEGY.md
  architecture/
  development/
  testing/internal/
  user-guide/internal/
  adr/
```

or create a separately generated external docs tree.

MkDocs itself describes `exclude_docs` as the mechanism for excluding files from the built site. citeturn361905search0turn361905search1

### And the test must change

RTM-62 should actually:

1. build the external site;
2. walk the resulting `site/`;
3. assert no internal HTML files exist;
4. optionally scan search index/output for internal text;
5. assert only approved external paths are published.

Until then:

> **Do not deploy the V018 external MkDocs build to customers.**

---

# 2. HIGH — ADR-018's core publication statement is therefore false

ADR-018 currently says:

> "`mkdocs-external.yml` publishes only customer-facing pages."

and:

> internal material is excluded by not being navigated to.

That is architecturally incorrect.

Because ADR-018 is the accepted decision defining the documentation security boundary, this is more than a configuration typo.

The correct design principle should be:

```text
INTERNAL source
        ↓
internal publication profile
        ↓
all approved internal + external docs

EXTERNAL source selection
        ↓
hard exclusion / allowlisted publication
        ↓
external docs only
```

For an external/customer-facing publication, I would favour **allowlisting** rather than relying only on denylisting.

For example:

```text
external publishable source:
user-guide/external/*
testing/external/*
```

Everything else is unpublished by default.

That is safer as the repository grows.

---

# 3. HIGH — RTM-62 claims more than its test proves

RTM says:

> **“The external documentation profile publishes no internal page.”**

The actual test checks:

> “the external `nav:` string doesn't contain an internal prefix.”

This is another occurrence of the exact pattern Claude itself documented in V017:

> **rule wording runs ahead of evidence.**

V018 says this lesson has become a governance rule, yet RTM-62 immediately violates it.

I'd either narrow RTM-62 temporarily to:

> “External navigation lists no internal page”

or, preferably, create the real built-site verification above.

# 16. The internal/external model should be stricter than “different nav”

I recommend this publication architecture:

```text
SOURCE REPOSITORY
│
├── internal-only docs
├── external docs
└── shared docs if any
         │
         ├──────── internal build
         │            ↓
         │        everything approved
         │
         └──────── external build
                      ↓
               explicit allowlist only
```

For a customer-facing documentation site:

> **absence from nav is not a security boundary.**

That should become an explicit architectural rule.

# 20. What I'd ask Claude to do for V019

Send Claude this:

> **V018 deep review complete. The testing architecture/manual-testing work is directionally very good, but V018 has one BLOCKER: the external documentation publication boundary is not real. Prepare V019 before any external docs are published.**
>
> **1. BLOCKER — `nav` is not a publication boundary in MkDocs.**
>
> ADR-018 and RTM-62 currently assume that leaving internal Markdown out of `mkdocs-external.yml` navigation prevents it being published. MkDocs builds Markdown pages not present in `nav`; they are merely hidden from navigation and remain directly reachable.
>
> Therefore the external build can still contain:
>
> - `ARCHITECTURE.md`
> - `TEST_STRATEGY.md`
> - `architecture/*`
> - `development/*`
> - `testing/internal/*`
> - `user-guide/internal/*`
> - `adr/*`
>
> This invalidates the current statement “external profile publishes only customer-facing pages.”
>
> Use a true publication boundary: preferably an explicit external allowlist or `exclude_docs`, not navigation hiding.
>
> **2. Replace RTM-62 with a real built-site test.**
>
> Build `mkdocs-external.yml` into a temporary directory and inspect output. Assert:
>
> - no internal HTML/page exists,
> - no internal ADR/architecture/development page exists,
> - internal terms/content do not enter the search index,
> - only approved external sources are present.
>
> The rule may say “publishes no internal page” only if the built output is inspected.
>
> **3. Correct ADR-018 Decision 4.**
>
> “Excluded by not being navigated to” is incorrect. Record the actual publication-selection mechanism.
>
> **4. Fix the broken internal testing-doc link.**
>
> `docs/testing/internal/00-index.md` links to nonexistent `07-manual-testing.md`.
>
> Create the page and use it as the web-facing index into MT-001–MT-004, or correct the link.
>
> **5. Strengthen RTM-63.**
>
> It currently verifies only MkDocs nav targets, while ADR-018 describes it as catching broken links. Either narrow its wording or add a real Markdown/MkDocs link-validation build in strict mode.
>
> **6. Fix current V018 path drift after the test restructure.**
>
> Replace current references:
>
> `tests/architecture/...`
>
> with:
>
> `tests/internal/architecture/...`
>
> and:
>
> `tests/integration/...`
>
> with:
>
> `tests/internal/integration/...`
>
> in current authoritative files including README, READ_THIS_FIRST and architecture.toml comments.
>
> Historical ADRs may retain original paths; add supersession notes if useful rather than rewriting history.
>
> **7. Slightly narrow EXT-UAT-001's claim.**
>
> A PASS shows the export is structurally accounted for with no reported parse issue and duplicate behaviour is correct. It does not by itself prove every parsed field is semantically correct. Align “what this confirms” with what the test actually examines.
>
> **8. Change external revocation wording.**
>
> Current code reports events as orphaned after final import revocation; it does not implement “marked for removal”. Use the actual term and explicitly state deletion is not implemented.
>
> **9. Add web-facing internal testing pages.**
>
> Keep detailed procedures under `tests/internal/manual`, but create navigable documentation pages for internal test areas, especially `07-manual-testing.md`, with purpose, audience, commands, links to scripts/procedures, and release expectations.
>
> **10. Preserve the good V018 work.**
>
> Keep `testing.toml`, MT-001–004, external synthetic UAT, planned-with-no-directory test levels, generated indexes, and data classification.
>
> Suggested next package:
>
> **`AT-LifeOperatingSystem_DocumentationPublicationBoundaryAndTestPortalIntegrity_Claude_V019_<YYYYMMDD_HHMM>.zip`**

| Area | Verdict |
|---|---|
| 100% line coverage | ✅ PASS |
| 100% branch coverage | ✅ PASS |
| Testing manifest | ✅ Strong |
| Internal/external testing concept | ✅ Strong |

| Product capability added | ✅ None, as intended |

## Bottom line

Your idea to create the **testing architecture now** was right. V018 has made a meaningful structural improvement and I would preserve most of it.

But there is one mistake that must be fixed before calling this a proper SaaS-style internal/external documentation system:

> **Hiding internal pages from the navigation is not the same as not publishing them.**

That is the central V019 correction. Once the external build is a real allowlisted/excluded publication, this testing/documentation framework will be much safer to grow with the project.

Your verdict was right. **V019 is a feature** — the first release since V008 to add capability rather than another control.

## `at-ingest-llm-export`

ChatGPT, Claude, Perplexity. Three genuinely different shapes: a node graph, an ordered list, query/answer pairs. **41 tests, 100% line and branch.**

**Each provider gets its own** **`origin_kind`****.** Not one `llm_export` — `origin_kind` participates in event identity, so a single kind would make the same conversation from two providers indistinguishable. Tested: identical content parsed as ChatGPT and as Claude yields **no overlapping event IDs**.

**Coverage moved to source units.** `line_coverage` was right for WhatsApp and meaningless for JSON — an export can be one line or pretty-printed across thousands. `line_coverage` now **raises** for a non-line source rather than returning a plausible number in the wrong unit. WhatsApp: *283 of 283 lines*. AI export: *4 of 4 messages*.

**Unknown shape raises**, never a partial parse. Detection is structural, so renaming a file can't change how it's parsed.

**Conversation boundaries survive** — ChatGPT nodes sorted by time then id so dictionary order can't decide message order; Perplexity's query and answer become two messages.

## The framework got used, not extended

Declaring the package in `architecture.toml` first made four guards fail until the code existed. That's V018 working as designed.

**5 of 11 packages, two independent source adapters** — which is what makes the generic `Event` contract a real contract rather than a WhatsApp shape with a general name.

```
python bootstrap.py
python -m at_cli acceptance tests/external/sample-data/sample_claude_export.json

```

One honest gap I've recorded: the adapter is tested against synthetic fixtures matching documented export shapes, not a genuine multi-gigabyte provider export. That's the next real-data test.

Next: `at-store` durability, then `at-identity`. `at-consent` blocked on DQ-012/DQ-013.

**AT-056 remains Not started.**
